What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I suppose I'm here because... my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. It's like a sieve lately. I sit down to write a simple note for the youth maker program I help out with... well, I used to help out more. The kids there, they're always building things with these little circuit boards. Raspberry something or other. When I got my bachelor's, we didn't have any of that. We had to actually draw out our schematics by hand on real drafting paper. I had this beautiful drafting table in my old office. Heavy oak. Took three men to move it when I finally retired. I think my nephew has it now in his garage, just gathering dust, which is a real shame because the wood grain on it was just spectacular. \n\nBut anyway, what was I... right, the notes. I sit down to write these simple notes for the program, something I could do in my sleep before, and the numbers and words just... they scatter on me. I just sit there staring at the page and I can't make them line up anymore.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice that your mind couldn't hold onto things like it used to?\n\nPatient: Well, it started about... I'd say three weeks ago. Right around the time my older sister passed away. We had to sort through all her things, you know? She lived in this big Victorian house over on Elm Street. Beautiful place, wrap-around porch, stained glass windows in the entryway. She was always so proud of those windows. Said they were original to the house, built in 1895. I remember when we were kids, she used to sit on that porch and read to me. She had this whole collection of Nancy Drew mysteries, the ones with the yellow spines. I wonder whatever happened to those books. Probably ended up in a box somewhere in the attic. Attics are terrible for books, the humidity just ruins the binding. I tried to tell my nephew that when he took my drafting table, but young people today, they don't listen. They just want everything digital. But yes, the... the fog in my head, it really settled in about three weeks ago, right after she died.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose you could say that. It's... well, most days I wake up and there's just this heavy, hollow feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like I'm stuck at the bottom of a deep well. Even when my wife is trying to talk to me, being so kind and patient... she really is a saint, my Martha. We met at a church social back in '78. She was wearing this lovely yellow dress, the color of buttercups. You don't see dresses made like that anymore, the stitching was impeccable. Her mother was a seamstress, actually. Worked down at the old textile mill before it closed in the 80s. That whole town changed when the mill shut down. It's mostly antique shops and coffee houses now. They charge four dollars for a cup of black coffee! Can you imagine? In my day, a cup of joe was a quarter, and the waitress would top it off for free. She always knew everyone's name, too. I think her name was Betty... or maybe Barbara? She had this big red hair, always wore it in a beehive... But yes, the emptiness. It's just... it's there all the time now. Like I'm just sitting in the dark at the bottom of that well, no matter what Martha says to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping from one thought to another without finishing the first one?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I jump around. I mean, not like those kids at the maker program do. They can't sit still for two minutes! Always bouncing from one gadget to the next. One minute they're soldering wires, the next they're looking at their phones. Everyone is always looking at their phones these days. I saw a man walk right into a lamppost downtown last week because his nose was buried in a screen. Just smack right into it! It was right in front of the old hardware store... the one that used to sell those beautiful brass hinges. You can't find good brass hardware anymore, it's all cheap metal painted to look like brass. It tarnishes in a year. I built a birdhouse back in '95, put real brass hinges on the roof so I could clean it out. Still looks brand new. The bluebirds just love it. Every spring they come back. But... no, I don't think my thoughts are jumping. They're just... they just get stuck, or I can't hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disorganized or hard to follow when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Disorganized... yes, I suppose that's a good word for it. It's exactly why I came in, really, because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I'll sit down to write a simple note for that youth maker program I mentioned—just basic instructions, something I could do in my sleep before. We use a lot of balsa wood for the models. Balsa is fascinating, you know. It's technically a hardwood, but it's incredibly light. We used to get it from Mr. Henderson's hobby shop on 4th Street. He had this old brass cash register that would make the most wonderful bell sound when he rang you up. Real brass, not like the cheap stuff I was talking about earlier. He kept a jar of butterscotch candies right next to it, the ones with the yellow cellophane wrappers. You don't see those much anymore, do you? Now everything is sour gummies or those energy drinks. The kids drink those energy drinks and they're practically vibrating. But yes, I try to write these simple notes for them, and the numbers and the words... they just completely scatter on me. I just can't keep them straight to explain anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Forget things recently? No, I don't... I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I can tell you the starting lineup of the '68 Tigers, no problem. Al Kaline in right field, what a player he was. They called him \"Mr. Tiger.\" You don't get nicknames like that anymore. Now it's all just initials and numbers, like they're robots or something. We used to listen to the games on this little transistor radio my dad bought at Sears. It had a leather case that smelled like... well, like real leather. Not that synthetic stuff they put on car seats now that cracks in the sun. I bought a car back in 2010, the seats started peeling after just three years. Terrible craftsmanship. They build things to throw away now, don't they? Planned obsolescence, they call it. But no, I haven't noticed any trouble with my memory. I remember things just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking or writing?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always had a pretty good vocabulary, really. I still do the Sunday crossword in the paper every week. In pen, too. You have to use a good pen for that, though. I have this old Parker fountain pen I got when I finished my bachelor's degree. The nib on it is just perfect, the ink flows so smooth. You can't use those cheap plastic ballpoints they give away at banks now. They always dry up, or worse, they leak right in your pocket. I ruined a perfectly good flannel shirt that way once. It was this thick red plaid one, my absolute favorite for raking leaves in the autumn. We had this massive oak tree at my old house, and the acorns would drop on the aluminum gutters and make just an awful racket. Sounded like someone was throwing marbles at the house. The squirrels loved it, though. They'd bury them all over the lawn. But, um... no, the words come to me just fine. I don't have any trouble finding them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing your attention on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Focus? Yes, that's exactly what I was trying to explain to you earlier. That's really the whole reason I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I'll be sitting there trying to focus on writing out those simple notes for the youth maker program—something I used to do in my sleep, like I said—and my attention just completely breaks. The numbers and the words just scatter on the page. We use a lot of graph paper for those notes, actually. The kind with the pale blue lines. I always preferred the quarter-inch grid. It reminds me of the old drafting tables we had when I was getting my bachelor's degree. They were these massive, heavy wooden things with those big metal swing-arm lamps attached to the side. The bulbs in those lamps would get so hot you could practically cook an egg on the metal casing. A fellow in my dorm, Richard, actually tried that once. Made a terrible, greasy mess of the whole desk. He was always trying crazy stunts like that instead of studying. Ended up moving to Chicago to work in advertising, I think. Advertising was such a wild business back in the seventies, all those billboards and magazine spreads. But, um... yes, if I try to sit and focus on one thing for a while, like those instructions, the thoughts just slip right through my fingers.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts of death or wish you were dead?\n\nPatient: Death? I... well, yes. I do think about that. I just keep thinking that maybe my family would be better off without me around, being like this. It gets really bad when I'm near my workshop. I get scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone in there with all the tools. I have a lot of tools out there. Hand saws, power drills, chisels. My favorite is this old Stanley block plane I found at a flea market about twenty years ago. The blade on it was rusted completely shut when I bought it, but I spent a whole weekend soaking it in vinegar and polishing it up. It takes a really nice edge now. I used it to smooth out the edges of a cedar chest I built for my daughter when she went off to college. Cedar smells so wonderful, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. Moths used to be a real problem in our old house. They got into my wife's wool winter coat and chewed these tiny little holes right through the lapel. She was so upset, she had bought that coat on a trip we took to Montreal. Montreal is beautiful in the winter, if you don't mind the cold. We went ice skating in this park... but anyway, yes, I get very scared of what I might do if I'm left alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Yes... I suppose I have. It's just... I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my workbench. They've just been gathering dust. And I've completely skipped out on the choir planning committee, and my weekly chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which really isn't like me at all. I used to love going down to the lake. We have this little aluminum boat, nothing fancy, just a flat-bottomed thing with a small outboard motor. It's painted this awful shade of olive green. The guy I bought it from said it was military surplus, but I think he just found a cheap can of paint at the hardware store. Hardware stores used to be so much better, didn't they? You could walk in and actually find someone who knew the difference between a carriage bolt and a lag screw. Now it's just teenagers in vests who don't know a wrench from a screwdriver. I bought a set of screwdrivers from one of those big box stores once, and the handle completely shattered the first time I tried to use it. Plastic just isn't made like it used to be. It was one of those clear yellow handles, you know the kind? They look like hard candy. But... um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I just don't have the energy or the desire for any of the things I used to enjoy doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble taking care of your daily responsibilities because of your memory and concentration issues?\n\nPatient: Responsibilities? Well... yes, I suppose I am. Everything's just... falling away. I've completely let my church duties slide, and the garden is an absolute mess right now. And that youth maker program I mentioned... I just can't do it. My wife and our pastor have actually had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. Especially because... well, like I said, I just don't feel safe being by myself out in the workshop anymore. It's a shame about the garden, really. I had planted these beautiful heirloom tomatoes this year. Brandywine, I think they were called. The seeds came in this little brown paper packet with a beautiful vintage illustration on the front. I love old illustrations like that, the kind you used to see in seed catalogs back in the fifties. My grandfather used to get those catalogs in the mail every spring. He lived out in Pennsylvania, had this massive plot of land where he grew mostly corn and soybeans. The soil out there was so dark and rich, it looked like coffee grounds. He used to make his own compost in this big wooden bin he built out of old shipping pallets. Pallets are incredibly useful if you know how to break them down properly. You have to be careful of the rusty nails, though. I stepped on a rusty nail when I was about ten years old, went right through my canvas sneaker. My mother had to take me to the clinic for a tetanus shot. I absolutely hated needles back then. Still do, if I'm being honest. The nurse gave me a lollipop afterward, one of those flat ones with the little loop handle... but, um, yes, my wife and the pastor have been handling things because I just can't keep up with my responsibilities right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner?\n\nPatient: Trouble expressing my thoughts? I... well, I don't know if I'd say that exactly. I mean, I can talk just fine, obviously. But sometimes it feels like my words get tangled up, or I start thinking about one thing and it just leads to another. Like the other day, my wife asked me what I wanted for dinner, and I started thinking about this pot roast my mother used to make. She had this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven. It was incredibly heavy, almost too heavy for her to lift when she got older. Cast iron is wonderful for cooking, though. It distributes the heat so evenly. You have to season it properly, of course. You rub it with oil and bake it in the oven. Some people say you should never use soap on cast iron, but I think a little bit of mild dish soap is fine as long as you dry it immediately. My grandmother used to use lye soap that she made herself. Can you imagine? Making your own soap. She used ashes from the fireplace and animal fat. It smelled awful, to be honest, but it got your hands incredibly clean. Especially after working in the garden. She always had dirt under her fingernails. She grew the most amazing hydrangeas. The soil acidity changes the color of the blooms, you know. If you put coffee grounds in the soil, they turn blue. Or maybe it's pink? I can never remember. I used to drink so much coffee when I worked at the firm. The coffee machine in the breakroom was always breaking down. It made this terrible gurgling noise... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, expressing my thoughts. I don't think I have trouble with it, really. I just... I have a lot on my mind, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: Disconnected? I don't really think so. My thoughts feel like they connect just fine to me, they just... travel a bit, I guess. Like a train moving from one car to the next. I've always loved trains, actually. When I was a boy, my uncle worked for the Union Pacific. He wore one of those striped caps, the classic engineer hats, you know? He gave me one for my seventh birthday. I wore it everywhere, even to church, until my mother finally hid it. She said it wasn't appropriate for Sunday service. Sunday services used to feel so much longer back then. The pews were made of this incredibly hard oak, and they polished them so much you'd slide right off if you weren't paying attention. The church had these beautiful stained glass windows, though. Real leaded glass, not the cheap painted stuff you see sometimes nowadays. The light would come through in the morning and cast these blue and red shadows across the floor. My favorite was the one showing Noah's ark. I always wondered how they fit all those animals in there. Especially the giraffes. They'd need a very tall ceiling, wouldn't they? And the smell must have been terrible. We went to a petting zoo once with our kids when they were little, and just a few goats and sheep smelled strong enough. One of the goats actually ate the map right out of my daughter's hand. She cried for an hour. We had to buy her a giant cotton candy to calm her down. Cotton candy is just spun sugar, really, it's amazing how they make it. The machine spins so fast... but, anyway, no, I don't feel like my thoughts are disconnected. They all make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Did your sister's passing trigger a significant change in how your mind works or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Change? Yes... yes, I suppose it did. This whole thing, it's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since my older sister died. We had her service on a Tuesday, and I remember it was raining so incredibly hard that morning. The wipers on my car could barely keep up. I still drive a '98 Buick LeSabre, you know. It's an absolute boat of a car, but the suspension is just incredibly smooth. They really don't make cars like that anymore. Everything nowadays is all computers and touchscreens. If something breaks, you can't even fix it yourself under the hood. My father taught me how to change the oil and the spark plugs when I was just sixteen. We had this old Ford pickup, cherry red. He used to keep a rag in his back pocket that always smelled like gasoline and motor grease. I actually loved that smell. It always reminds me of summer afternoons in the garage with the radio playing. We used to listen to the baseball games on this little silver transistor radio he had. The announcer's voice would crackle through the static every time a truck drove by outside. Baseball was so different back then, the games just felt more... I don't know, romantic? Now they have that new pitch clock, which I suppose is fine for television, but it feels a bit rushed, doesn't it? I always liked the slow pace of the game, sitting out in the bleachers with a hot dog in the sun. The yellow mustard they use at the stadium is always so much better than the kind you buy in the bottles at the grocery store. I think it's because they keep it in those big warm dispensers, or maybe it's the vinegar ratio... but, anyway, yes, it's been about three weeks since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your family or the people around you?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... well, yes, honestly. Lately, I keep thinking my family might just be better off without me around to worry about. It's a terrible thought, but it just sits there in my head. Especially when I'm out near my workshop. I've been spending less time out there because... well, I get scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone with all my tools. I have a lot of tools out there. My grandfather's old lathe is in the corner. It's a beautiful piece of machinery, heavy cast iron, just like that Dutch oven I was telling you about. You can't move it without three strong men and a dolly. I used to turn the most wonderful wooden bowls on that lathe. Cherry wood, mostly. Cherry has such a tight grain, it finishes so smoothly if you use the right grit sandpaper. You have to work your way up, you know, from 80 grit all the way to 400, sometimes 600 if you want a real glass-like finish. Then you rub it with beeswax and mineral oil. The smell of sawdust and beeswax is one of my favorite things in the world. It smells like... like accomplishment, I suppose. I made a salad bowl for my wife on our twentieth anniversary. She still uses it every Thanksgiving. Thanksgiving is going to be so strange this year without my sister. She always made the cranberry sauce from scratch. Never the canned stuff. The canned stuff has those ridges on the side from the tin, and it jiggles like gelatin. I never understood why people like that. Real cranberries are so tart, you have to add just the right amount of sugar and orange zest... but, yes, a burden. I do feel like that. And those thoughts about the workshop... they frighten me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury that caused you to lose consciousness or experience confusion?\n\nPatient: A head injury? No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that. I've bumped my head a few times, sure, but never blacked out or anything. The worst I ever got was a scrape on my forehead when I was helping my neighbor, Tom, take down this old oak tree in his front yard. It was a massive tree, must have been at least eighty years old. The roots were starting to crack his driveway, pushing up the concrete in these big jagged chunks. We were out there with chainsaws for two whole days. Chainsaws can be so temperamental if you don't mix the gas and oil just right. It's a 50-to-1 ratio for most of the newer ones, but the older models sometimes needed 40-to-1. If you get it wrong, the engine just smokes like crazy or seizes up entirely. Tom had this old Stihl chainsaw that his father had given him. It was heavy as a rock, but it cut through that oak like butter. Oak is such a dense wood, it's terrible for dulling your blades. You have to stop and file the teeth on the chain every few hours if you want to keep a clean cut. I actually find sharpening chains quite relaxing. You have to get the file at exactly a 30-degree angle, and just do smooth, even strokes. It takes patience. My wife always said I had too much patience for things like that, and not enough for... well, for other things. But anyway, no, I've never lost consciousness or anything from a head injury. Just a few bumps and scrapes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Stick to one topic? I... well, I haven't really noticed that, no. Do you think I do? I suppose sometimes my mind just wanders a bit. It's like when I'm trying to organize my workbench. I'll go out there with the intention of sorting all the loose screws into those little plastic bins. You know the ones, they have the clear drawers so you can see exactly what's inside. I bought a whole set of them at the hardware store a few years back. The hardware store downtown, not the big box one out on the highway. The old one has those creaky wooden floors that slope a little towards the back. They still sell nails by the pound out of wooden barrels. It's fantastic. You just take a paper bag and scoop them in. The paper bags always have that distinct smell, kind of dusty and dry. I used to love going there on Saturday mornings to pick up supplies for my projects. They had a shop cat, too, a big orange tabby named Barnaby. He used to sleep right on top of the bags of potting soil. Cats are so funny about where they decide to sleep. They'll ignore a perfectly good bed and just curl up in a cardboard box. We had a cat when the kids were young, a little calico named Patches. She used to chase the laser pointer until she was completely out of breath. My son loved doing that... but anyway, no, I don't think I have a hard time sticking to a topic. Not that I've noticed, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: Changes in my sleep? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sleeping great, but it hasn't really changed much lately. I still try to get into bed around ten. That's when the local news finishes. I always liked watching the local news, just to see what the weather is going to do. The weatherman on Channel 4, he's been there for at least twenty years. He always wears these incredibly loud ties. Last week he had one on that looked like a whole flock of flamingos. Flamingos are fascinating birds, really. Did you know they only turn pink because of the shrimp they eat? If they don't eat the shrimp, their feathers just stay kind of a dull gray. It's all about their diet. Diet is so important, especially as you get older. My wife keeps trying to get me to eat more leafy greens. She bought this huge bag of kale the other day. Kale is so tough, though. You really have to massage it with olive oil if you want it to be edible in a salad. Otherwise, it's like chewing on tree bark. I tried growing kale in my garden once, but the aphids got to it before it even got knee-high. Aphids are a nightmare to get rid of. You can use ladybugs, though. You can actually order a box of live ladybugs through the mail and just release them in your garden. It's the most bizarre thing, getting a cardboard box full of bugs delivered by the mailman... but, no, my sleep is about the same. Nothing really new there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It was incredibly heavy, almost too heavy for her to lift when she got older. Cast iron is wonderful for cooking, though. It distributes the heat so evenly. You have to season it properly, of course. You rub it with oil and bake it in the oven. Some people say you should never use soap on cast iron, but I think a little bit of mild dish soap is fine as long as you dry it immediately. My grandmother used to use lye soap that she made herself. Can you imagine? Making your own soap. She used ashes from the fireplace and animal fat. It smelled awful, to be honest, but it got your hands incredibly clean. Especially after working in the garden. She always had dirt under her fingernails. She grew the most amazing hydrangeas. The soil acidity changes the color of the blooms, you know. If you put coffee grounds in the soil, they turn blue. Or maybe it's pink? I can never remember. I used to drink so much coffee when I worked at the firm. The coffee machine in the breakroom was always breaking down. It made this terrible gurgling noise... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, expressing my thoughts. I don't think I have trouble with it, really. I just... I have a lot on my mind, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: Disconnected? I don't really think so. My thoughts feel like they connect just fine to me, they just... travel a bit, I guess. Like a train moving from one car to the next. I've always loved trains, actually. When I was a boy, my uncle worked for the Union Pacific. He wore one of those striped caps, the classic engineer hats, you know? He gave me one for my seventh birthday. I wore it everywhere, even to church, until my mother finally hid it. She said it wasn't appropriate for Sunday service. Sunday services used to feel so much longer back then. The pews were made of this incredibly hard oak, and they polished them so much you'd slide right off if you weren't paying attention. The church had these beautiful stained glass windows, though. Real leaded glass, not the cheap painted stuff you see sometimes nowadays. The light would come through in the morning and cast these blue and red shadows across the floor. My favorite was the one showing Noah's ark. I always wondered how they fit all those animals in there. Especially the giraffes. They'd need a very tall ceiling, wouldn't they? And the smell must have been terrible. We went to a petting zoo once with our kids when they were little, and just a few goats and sheep smelled strong enough. One of the goats actually ate the map right out of my daughter's hand. She cried for an hour. We had to buy her a giant cotton candy to calm her down. Cotton candy is just spun sugar, really, it's amazing how they make it. The machine spins so fast... but, anyway, no, I don't feel like my thoughts are disconnected. They all make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Did your sister's passing trigger a significant change in how your mind works or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Change? Yes... yes, I suppose it did. This whole thing, it's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since my older sister died. We had her service on a Tuesday, and I remember it was raining so incredibly hard that morning. The wipers on my car could barely keep up. I still drive a '98 Buick LeSabre, you know. It's an absolute boat of a car, but the suspension is just incredibly smooth. They really don't make cars like that anymore. Everything nowadays is all computers and touchscreens. If something breaks, you can't even fix it yourself under the hood. My father taught me how to change the oil and the spark plugs when I was just sixteen. We had this old Ford pickup, cherry red. He used to keep a rag in his back pocket that always smelled like gasoline and motor grease. I actually loved that smell. It always reminds me of summer afternoons in the garage with the radio playing. We used to listen to the baseball games on this little silver transistor radio he had. The announcer's voice would crackle through the static every time a truck drove by outside. Baseball was so different back then, the games just felt more... I don't know, romantic? Now they have that new pitch clock, which I suppose is fine for television, but it feels a bit rushed, doesn't it? I always liked the slow pace of the game, sitting out in the bleachers with a hot dog in the sun. The yellow mustard they use at the stadium is always so much better than the kind you buy in the bottles at the grocery store. I think it's because they keep it in those big warm dispensers, or maybe it's the vinegar ratio... but, anyway, yes, it's been about three weeks since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your family or the people around you?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... well, yes, honestly. Lately, I keep thinking my family might just be better off without me around to worry about. It's a terrible thought, but it just sits there in my head. Especially when I'm out near my workshop. I've been spending less time out there because... well, I get scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone with all my tools. I have a lot of tools out there. My grandfather's old lathe is in the corner. It's a beautiful piece of machinery, heavy cast iron, just like that Dutch oven I was telling you about. You can't move it without three strong men and a dolly. I used to turn the most wonderful wooden bowls on that lathe. Cherry wood, mostly. Cherry has such a tight grain, it finishes so smoothly if you use the right grit sandpaper. You have to work your way up, you know, from 80 grit all the way to 400, sometimes 600 if you want a real glass-like finish. Then you rub it with beeswax and mineral oil. The smell of sawdust and beeswax is one of my favorite things in the world. It smells like... like accomplishment, I suppose. I made a salad bowl for my wife on our twentieth anniversary. She still uses it every Thanksgiving. Thanksgiving is going to be so strange this year without my sister. She always made the cranberry sauce from scratch. Never the canned stuff. The canned stuff has those ridges on the side from the tin, and it jiggles like gelatin. I never understood why people like that. Real cranberries are so tart, you have to add just the right amount of sugar and orange zest... but, yes, a burden. I do feel like that. And those thoughts about the workshop... they frighten me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury that caused you to lose consciousness or experience confusion?\n\nPatient: A head injury? No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that. I've bumped my head a few times, sure, but never blacked out or anything. The worst I ever got was a scrape on my forehead when I was helping my neighbor, Tom, take down this old oak tree in his front yard. It was a massive tree, must have been at least eighty years old. The roots were starting to crack his driveway, pushing up the concrete in these big jagged chunks. We were out there with chainsaws for two whole days. Chainsaws can be so temperamental if you don't mix the gas and oil just right. It's a 50-to-1 ratio for most of the newer ones, but the older models sometimes needed 40-to-1. If you get it wrong, the engine just smokes like crazy or seizes up entirely. Tom had this old Stihl chainsaw that his father had given him. It was heavy as a rock, but it cut through that oak like butter. Oak is such a dense wood, it's terrible for dulling your blades. You have to stop and file the teeth on the chain every few hours if you want to keep a clean cut. I actually find sharpening chains quite relaxing. You have to get the file at exactly a 30-degree angle, and just do smooth, even strokes. It takes patience. My wife always said I had too much patience for things like that, and not enough for... well, for other things. But anyway, no, I've never lost consciousness or anything from a head injury. Just a few bumps and scrapes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Stick to one topic? I... well, I haven't really noticed that, no. Do you think I do? I suppose sometimes my mind just wanders a bit. It's like when I'm trying to organize my workbench. I'll go out there with the intention of sorting all the loose screws into those little plastic bins. You know the ones, they have the clear drawers so you can see exactly what's inside. I bought a whole set of them at the hardware store a few years back. The hardware store downtown, not the big box one out on the highway. The old one has those creaky wooden floors that slope a little towards the back. They still sell nails by the pound out of wooden barrels. It's fantastic. You just take a paper bag and scoop them in. The paper bags always have that distinct smell, kind of dusty and dry. I used to love going there on Saturday mornings to pick up supplies for my projects. They had a shop cat, too, a big orange tabby named Barnaby. He used to sleep right on top of the bags of potting soil. Cats are so funny about where they decide to sleep. They'll ignore a perfectly good bed and just curl up in a cardboard box. We had a cat when the kids were young, a little calico named Patches. She used to chase the laser pointer until she was completely out of breath. My son loved doing that... but anyway, no, I don't think I have a hard time sticking to a topic. Not that I've noticed, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: Changes in my sleep? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sleeping great, but it hasn't really changed much lately. I still try to get into bed around ten. That's when the local news finishes. I always liked watching the local news, just to see what the weather is going to do. The weatherman on Channel 4, he's been there for at least twenty years. He always wears these incredibly loud ties. Last week he had one on that looked like a whole flock of flamingos. Flamingos are fascinating birds, really. Did you know they only turn pink because of the shrimp they eat? If they don't eat the shrimp, their feathers just stay kind of a dull gray. It's all about their diet. Diet is so important, especially as you get older. My wife keeps trying to get me to eat more leafy greens. She bought this huge bag of kale the other day. Kale is so tough, though. You really have to massage it with olive oil if you want it to be edible in a salad. Otherwise, it's like chewing on tree bark. I tried growing kale in my garden once, but the aphids got to it before it even got knee-high. Aphids are a nightmare to get rid of. You can use ladybugs, though. You can actually order a box of live ladybugs through the mail and just release them in your garden. It's the most bizarre thing, getting a cardboard box full of bugs delivered by the mailman... but, no, my sleep is about the same. Nothing really new there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Searching for the right word? I... no, I haven't really noticed that being a problem. I mean, sometimes I pause to think, but I don't think I'm losing my words. It's not like what happened to my Uncle Arthur. He had a terrible time with words toward the end. He used to do the crossword puzzle in the Sunday paper every single week, in pen. The New York Times one, the really difficult one. I could never get past the first few clues, but he would just breeze through it while drinking his black coffee. He always drank it out of this chipped blue mug that he got from a diner in Maine. We used to go up to Maine every summer when I was a boy. The water up there is freezing, even in August. You run in and your legs go completely numb after about ten seconds. We'd collect these little hermit crabs in plastic buckets and watch them scurry around. Hermit crabs are so strange, the way they just steal other shells when they outgrow their old ones. Imagine if people did that. Just moving into someone else's house because your shoes got too tight. The housing market is crazy enough as it is right now. My daughter was looking at a place across town, and they wanted an absolute fortune for a house that didn't even have a finished basement. Basements are so important for storage. I keep all my old woodworking tools down there... but no, I don't think I'm having trouble finding words. I seem to be able to say what I mean well enough.

Beautiful place, wrap-around porch, stained glass windows in the entryway. She was always so proud of those windows. Said they were original to the house, built in 1895. I remember when we were kids, she used to sit on that porch and read to me. She had this whole collection of Nancy Drew mysteries, the ones with the yellow spines. I wonder whatever happened to those books. Probably ended up in a box somewhere in the attic. Attics are terrible for books, the humidity just ruins the binding. I tried to tell my nephew that when he took my drafting table, but young people today, they don't listen. They just want everything digital. But yes, the... the fog in my head, it really settled in about three weeks ago, right after she died.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or empty since your sister passed away?\n\nPatient: Yes, I suppose you could say that. It's... well, most days I wake up and there's just this heavy, hollow feeling sitting right here in my chest. It's like I'm stuck at the bottom of a deep well. Even when my wife is trying to talk to me, being so kind and patient... she really is a saint, my Martha. We met at a church social back in '78. She was wearing this lovely yellow dress, the color of buttercups. You don't see dresses made like that anymore, the stitching was impeccable. Her mother was a seamstress, actually. Worked down at the old textile mill before it closed in the 80s. That whole town changed when the mill shut down. It's mostly antique shops and coffee houses now. They charge four dollars for a cup of black coffee! Can you imagine? In my day, a cup of joe was a quarter, and the waitress would top it off for free. She always knew everyone's name, too. I think her name was Betty... or maybe Barbara? She had this big red hair, always wore it in a beehive... But yes, the emptiness. It's just... it's there all the time now. Like I'm just sitting in the dark at the bottom of that well, no matter what Martha says to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself jumping from one thought to another without finishing the first one?\n\nPatient: Well, I... I wouldn't say I jump around. I mean, not like those kids at the maker program do. They can't sit still for two minutes! Always bouncing from one gadget to the next. One minute they're soldering wires, the next they're looking at their phones. Everyone is always looking at their phones these days. I saw a man walk right into a lamppost downtown last week because his nose was buried in a screen. Just smack right into it! It was right in front of the old hardware store... the one that used to sell those beautiful brass hinges. You can't find good brass hardware anymore, it's all cheap metal painted to look like brass. It tarnishes in a year. I built a birdhouse back in '95, put real brass hinges on the roof so I could clean it out. Still looks brand new. The bluebirds just love it. Every spring they come back. But... no, I don't think my thoughts are jumping. They're just... they just get stuck, or I can't hold onto them.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disorganized or hard to follow when you're trying to explain something?\n\nPatient: Disorganized... yes, I suppose that's a good word for it. It's exactly why I came in, really, because my mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I'll sit down to write a simple note for that youth maker program I mentioned—just basic instructions, something I could do in my sleep before. We use a lot of balsa wood for the models. Balsa is fascinating, you know. It's technically a hardwood, but it's incredibly light. We used to get it from Mr. Henderson's hobby shop on 4th Street. He had this old brass cash register that would make the most wonderful bell sound when he rang you up. Real brass, not like the cheap stuff I was talking about earlier. He kept a jar of butterscotch candies right next to it, the ones with the yellow cellophane wrappers. You don't see those much anymore, do you? Now everything is sour gummies or those energy drinks. The kids drink those energy drinks and they're practically vibrating. But yes, I try to write these simple notes for them, and the numbers and the words... they just completely scatter on me. I just can't keep them straight to explain anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget things that happened recently?\n\nPatient: Forget things recently? No, I don't... I don't think so. My memory is sharp as a tack, usually. I can tell you the starting lineup of the '68 Tigers, no problem. Al Kaline in right field, what a player he was. They called him \"Mr. Tiger.\" You don't get nicknames like that anymore. Now it's all just initials and numbers, like they're robots or something. We used to listen to the games on this little transistor radio my dad bought at Sears. It had a leather case that smelled like... well, like real leather. Not that synthetic stuff they put on car seats now that cracks in the sun. I bought a car back in 2010, the seats started peeling after just three years. Terrible craftsmanship. They build things to throw away now, don't they? Planned obsolescence, they call it. But no, I haven't noticed any trouble with my memory. I remember things just fine.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself struggling to find the right words when you're talking or writing?\n\nPatient: Struggling to find words? No, I wouldn't say that. I've always had a pretty good vocabulary, really. I still do the Sunday crossword in the paper every week. In pen, too. You have to use a good pen for that, though. I have this old Parker fountain pen I got when I finished my bachelor's degree. The nib on it is just perfect, the ink flows so smooth. You can't use those cheap plastic ballpoints they give away at banks now. They always dry up, or worse, they leak right in your pocket. I ruined a perfectly good flannel shirt that way once. It was this thick red plaid one, my absolute favorite for raking leaves in the autumn. We had this massive oak tree at my old house, and the acorns would drop on the aluminum gutters and make just an awful racket. Sounded like someone was throwing marbles at the house. The squirrels loved it, though. They'd bury them all over the lawn. But, um... no, the words come to me just fine. I don't have any trouble finding them.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing your attention on one thing for a long period of time?\n\nPatient: Focus? Yes, that's exactly what I was trying to explain to you earlier. That's really the whole reason I came in. My mind just will not hold onto things anymore. I'll be sitting there trying to focus on writing out those simple notes for the youth maker program—something I used to do in my sleep, like I said—and my attention just completely breaks. The numbers and the words just scatter on the page. We use a lot of graph paper for those notes, actually. The kind with the pale blue lines. I always preferred the quarter-inch grid. It reminds me of the old drafting tables we had when I was getting my bachelor's degree. They were these massive, heavy wooden things with those big metal swing-arm lamps attached to the side. The bulbs in those lamps would get so hot you could practically cook an egg on the metal casing. A fellow in my dorm, Richard, actually tried that once. Made a terrible, greasy mess of the whole desk. He was always trying crazy stunts like that instead of studying. Ended up moving to Chicago to work in advertising, I think. Advertising was such a wild business back in the seventies, all those billboards and magazine spreads. But, um... yes, if I try to sit and focus on one thing for a while, like those instructions, the thoughts just slip right through my fingers.\n\nDoctor: Do you have thoughts of death or wish you were dead?\n\nPatient: Death? I... well, yes. I do think about that. I just keep thinking that maybe my family would be better off without me around, being like this. It gets really bad when I'm near my workshop. I get scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone in there with all the tools. I have a lot of tools out there. Hand saws, power drills, chisels. My favorite is this old Stanley block plane I found at a flea market about twenty years ago. The blade on it was rusted completely shut when I bought it, but I spent a whole weekend soaking it in vinegar and polishing it up. It takes a really nice edge now. I used it to smooth out the edges of a cedar chest I built for my daughter when she went off to college. Cedar smells so wonderful, doesn't it? It keeps the moths away, too. Moths used to be a real problem in our old house. They got into my wife's wool winter coat and chewed these tiny little holes right through the lapel. She was so upset, she had bought that coat on a trip we took to Montreal. Montreal is beautiful in the winter, if you don't mind the cold. We went ice skating in this park... but anyway, yes, I get very scared of what I might do if I'm left alone with my thoughts.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Interest? Yes... I suppose I have. It's just... I haven't even touched the wood pieces sitting on my workbench. They've just been gathering dust. And I've completely skipped out on the choir planning committee, and my weekly chess games. Even fishing... fishing just sounds like more effort than it's worth right now, which really isn't like me at all. I used to love going down to the lake. We have this little aluminum boat, nothing fancy, just a flat-bottomed thing with a small outboard motor. It's painted this awful shade of olive green. The guy I bought it from said it was military surplus, but I think he just found a cheap can of paint at the hardware store. Hardware stores used to be so much better, didn't they? You could walk in and actually find someone who knew the difference between a carriage bolt and a lag screw. Now it's just teenagers in vests who don't know a wrench from a screwdriver. I bought a set of screwdrivers from one of those big box stores once, and the handle completely shattered the first time I tried to use it. Plastic just isn't made like it used to be. It was one of those clear yellow handles, you know the kind? They look like hard candy. But... um, what was I saying? Oh, right. Yes, I just don't have the energy or the desire for any of the things I used to enjoy doing.\n\nDoctor: Are you having trouble taking care of your daily responsibilities because of your memory and concentration issues?\n\nPatient: Responsibilities? Well... yes, I suppose I am. Everything's just... falling away. I've completely let my church duties slide, and the garden is an absolute mess right now. And that youth maker program I mentioned... I just can't do it. My wife and our pastor have actually had to step in and take over a lot of things for me. Especially because... well, like I said, I just don't feel safe being by myself out in the workshop anymore. It's a shame about the garden, really. I had planted these beautiful heirloom tomatoes this year. Brandywine, I think they were called. The seeds came in this little brown paper packet with a beautiful vintage illustration on the front. I love old illustrations like that, the kind you used to see in seed catalogs back in the fifties. My grandfather used to get those catalogs in the mail every spring. He lived out in Pennsylvania, had this massive plot of land where he grew mostly corn and soybeans. The soil out there was so dark and rich, it looked like coffee grounds. He used to make his own compost in this big wooden bin he built out of old shipping pallets. Pallets are incredibly useful if you know how to break them down properly. You have to be careful of the rusty nails, though. I stepped on a rusty nail when I was about ten years old, went right through my canvas sneaker. My mother had to take me to the clinic for a tetanus shot. I absolutely hated needles back then. Still do, if I'm being honest. The nurse gave me a lollipop afterward, one of those flat ones with the little loop handle... but, um, yes, my wife and the pastor have been handling things because I just can't keep up with my responsibilities right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself having trouble expressing your thoughts in a coherent and logical manner?\n\nPatient: Trouble expressing my thoughts? I... well, I don't know if I'd say that exactly. I mean, I can talk just fine, obviously. But sometimes it feels like my words get tangled up, or I start thinking about one thing and it just leads to another. Like the other day, my wife asked me what I wanted for dinner, and I started thinking about this pot roast my mother used to make. She had this heavy cast-iron Dutch oven. It was incredibly heavy, almost too heavy for her to lift when she got older. Cast iron is wonderful for cooking, though. It distributes the heat so evenly. You have to season it properly, of course. You rub it with oil and bake it in the oven. Some people say you should never use soap on cast iron, but I think a little bit of mild dish soap is fine as long as you dry it immediately. My grandmother used to use lye soap that she made herself. Can you imagine? Making your own soap. She used ashes from the fireplace and animal fat. It smelled awful, to be honest, but it got your hands incredibly clean. Especially after working in the garden. She always had dirt under her fingernails. She grew the most amazing hydrangeas. The soil acidity changes the color of the blooms, you know. If you put coffee grounds in the soil, they turn blue. Or maybe it's pink? I can never remember. I used to drink so much coffee when I worked at the firm. The coffee machine in the breakroom was always breaking down. It made this terrible gurgling noise... but, um, what were we talking about? Oh, expressing my thoughts. I don't think I have trouble with it, really. I just... I have a lot on my mind, I suppose.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other?\n\nPatient: Disconnected? I don't really think so. My thoughts feel like they connect just fine to me, they just... travel a bit, I guess. Like a train moving from one car to the next. I've always loved trains, actually. When I was a boy, my uncle worked for the Union Pacific. He wore one of those striped caps, the classic engineer hats, you know? He gave me one for my seventh birthday. I wore it everywhere, even to church, until my mother finally hid it. She said it wasn't appropriate for Sunday service. Sunday services used to feel so much longer back then. The pews were made of this incredibly hard oak, and they polished them so much you'd slide right off if you weren't paying attention. The church had these beautiful stained glass windows, though. Real leaded glass, not the cheap painted stuff you see sometimes nowadays. The light would come through in the morning and cast these blue and red shadows across the floor. My favorite was the one showing Noah's ark. I always wondered how they fit all those animals in there. Especially the giraffes. They'd need a very tall ceiling, wouldn't they? And the smell must have been terrible. We went to a petting zoo once with our kids when they were little, and just a few goats and sheep smelled strong enough. One of the goats actually ate the map right out of my daughter's hand. She cried for an hour. We had to buy her a giant cotton candy to calm her down. Cotton candy is just spun sugar, really, it's amazing how they make it. The machine spins so fast... but, anyway, no, I don't feel like my thoughts are disconnected. They all make sense to me.\n\nDoctor: Did your sister's passing trigger a significant change in how your mind works or how you feel about yourself?\n\nPatient: Change? Yes... yes, I suppose it did. This whole thing, it's been going on for about three weeks now, ever since my older sister died. We had her service on a Tuesday, and I remember it was raining so incredibly hard that morning. The wipers on my car could barely keep up. I still drive a '98 Buick LeSabre, you know. It's an absolute boat of a car, but the suspension is just incredibly smooth. They really don't make cars like that anymore. Everything nowadays is all computers and touchscreens. If something breaks, you can't even fix it yourself under the hood. My father taught me how to change the oil and the spark plugs when I was just sixteen. We had this old Ford pickup, cherry red. He used to keep a rag in his back pocket that always smelled like gasoline and motor grease. I actually loved that smell. It always reminds me of summer afternoons in the garage with the radio playing. We used to listen to the baseball games on this little silver transistor radio he had. The announcer's voice would crackle through the static every time a truck drove by outside. Baseball was so different back then, the games just felt more... I don't know, romantic? Now they have that new pitch clock, which I suppose is fine for television, but it feels a bit rushed, doesn't it? I always liked the slow pace of the game, sitting out in the bleachers with a hot dog in the sun. The yellow mustard they use at the stadium is always so much better than the kind you buy in the bottles at the grocery store. I think it's because they keep it in those big warm dispensers, or maybe it's the vinegar ratio... but, anyway, yes, it's been about three weeks since she passed.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're a burden to your family or the people around you?\n\nPatient: A burden? I... well, yes, honestly. Lately, I keep thinking my family might just be better off without me around to worry about. It's a terrible thought, but it just sits there in my head. Especially when I'm out near my workshop. I've been spending less time out there because... well, I get scared of where my thoughts could go if I were left alone with all my tools. I have a lot of tools out there. My grandfather's old lathe is in the corner. It's a beautiful piece of machinery, heavy cast iron, just like that Dutch oven I was telling you about. You can't move it without three strong men and a dolly. I used to turn the most wonderful wooden bowls on that lathe. Cherry wood, mostly. Cherry has such a tight grain, it finishes so smoothly if you use the right grit sandpaper. You have to work your way up, you know, from 80 grit all the way to 400, sometimes 600 if you want a real glass-like finish. Then you rub it with beeswax and mineral oil. The smell of sawdust and beeswax is one of my favorite things in the world. It smells like... like accomplishment, I suppose. I made a salad bowl for my wife on our twentieth anniversary. She still uses it every Thanksgiving. Thanksgiving is going to be so strange this year without my sister. She always made the cranberry sauce from scratch. Never the canned stuff. The canned stuff has those ridges on the side from the tin, and it jiggles like gelatin. I never understood why people like that. Real cranberries are so tart, you have to add just the right amount of sugar and orange zest... but, yes, a burden. I do feel like that. And those thoughts about the workshop... they frighten me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a head injury that caused you to lose consciousness or experience confusion?\n\nPatient: A head injury? No, I don't think I've ever had anything like that. I've bumped my head a few times, sure, but never blacked out or anything. The worst I ever got was a scrape on my forehead when I was helping my neighbor, Tom, take down this old oak tree in his front yard. It was a massive tree, must have been at least eighty years old. The roots were starting to crack his driveway, pushing up the concrete in these big jagged chunks. We were out there with chainsaws for two whole days. Chainsaws can be so temperamental if you don't mix the gas and oil just right. It's a 50-to-1 ratio for most of the newer ones, but the older models sometimes needed 40-to-1. If you get it wrong, the engine just smokes like crazy or seizes up entirely. Tom had this old Stihl chainsaw that his father had given him. It was heavy as a rock, but it cut through that oak like butter. Oak is such a dense wood, it's terrible for dulling your blades. You have to stop and file the teeth on the chain every few hours if you want to keep a clean cut. I actually find sharpening chains quite relaxing. You have to get the file at exactly a 30-degree angle, and just do smooth, even strokes. It takes patience. My wife always said I had too much patience for things like that, and not enough for... well, for other things. But anyway, no, I've never lost consciousness or anything from a head injury. Just a few bumps and scrapes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find it hard to stick to one topic when talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Stick to one topic? I... well, I haven't really noticed that, no. Do you think I do? I suppose sometimes my mind just wanders a bit. It's like when I'm trying to organize my workbench. I'll go out there with the intention of sorting all the loose screws into those little plastic bins. You know the ones, they have the clear drawers so you can see exactly what's inside. I bought a whole set of them at the hardware store a few years back. The hardware store downtown, not the big box one out on the highway. The old one has those creaky wooden floors that slope a little towards the back. They still sell nails by the pound out of wooden barrels. It's fantastic. You just take a paper bag and scoop them in. The paper bags always have that distinct smell, kind of dusty and dry. I used to love going there on Saturday mornings to pick up supplies for my projects. They had a shop cat, too, a big orange tabby named Barnaby. He used to sleep right on top of the bags of potting soil. Cats are so funny about where they decide to sleep. They'll ignore a perfectly good bed and just curl up in a cardboard box. We had a cat when the kids were young, a little calico named Patches. She used to chase the laser pointer until she was completely out of breath. My son loved doing that... but anyway, no, I don't think I have a hard time sticking to a topic. Not that I've noticed, at least.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your sleep recently?\n\nPatient: Changes in my sleep? I don't... I don't really think so. I mean, I'm not sleeping great, but it hasn't really changed much lately. I still try to get into bed around ten. That's when the local news finishes. I always liked watching the local news, just to see what the weather is going to do. The weatherman on Channel 4, he's been there for at least twenty years. He always wears these incredibly loud ties. Last week he had one on that looked like a whole flock of flamingos. Flamingos are fascinating birds, really. Did you know they only turn pink because of the shrimp they eat? If they don't eat the shrimp, their feathers just stay kind of a dull gray. It's all about their diet. Diet is so important, especially as you get older. My wife keeps trying to get me to eat more leafy greens. She bought this huge bag of kale the other day. Kale is so tough, though. You really have to massage it with olive oil if you want it to be edible in a salad. Otherwise, it's like chewing on tree bark. I tried growing kale in my garden once, but the aphids got to it before it even got knee-high. Aphids are a nightmare to get rid of. You can use ladybugs, though. You can actually order a box of live ladybugs through the mail and just release them in your garden. It's the most bizarre thing, getting a cardboard box full of bugs delivered by the mailman... but, no, my sleep is about the same. Nothing really new there.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself often searching for the right word when trying to express your thoughts?\n\nPatient: Searching for the right word? I... no, I haven't really noticed that being a problem. I mean, sometimes I pause to think, but I don't think I'm losing my words. It's not like what happened to my Uncle Arthur. He had a terrible time with words toward the end. He used to do the crossword puzzle in the Sunday paper every single week, in pen. The New York Times one, the really difficult one. I could never get past the first few clues, but he would just breeze through it while drinking his black coffee. He always drank it out of this chipped blue mug that he got from a diner in Maine. We used to go up to Maine every summer when I was a boy. The water up there is freezing, even in August. You run in and your legs go completely numb after about ten seconds. We'd collect these little hermit crabs in plastic buckets and watch them scurry around. Hermit crabs are so strange, the way they just steal other shells when they outgrow their old ones. Imagine if people did that. Just moving into someone else's house because your shoes got too tight. The housing market is crazy enough as it is right now. My daughter was looking at a place across town, and they wanted an absolute fortune for a house that didn't even have a finished basement. Basements are so important for storage. I keep all my old woodworking tools down there... but no, I don't think I'm having trouble finding words. I seem to be able to say what I mean well enough.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.
